4l. In catena of judgements, it is held that the loss of confidence is the primary factor and not the amount of 
money misappropriated and that the sympathy or generosity cannot be a factor which is impermissible in law. When 
an employee is found guilty of misappropriating the Corporation’s funds, there is nothing wrong in the corporation 
losing confidence or faith in such an Employee and awarding punishment of Dismissal. In such cases, there is no 
place for generosity or misplaced sympathy on the part of the judicial forums and interfering therefore with the 
quantum of punishment. It is for the Management to maintain internal discipline, and particularly financial discipline, 
to keep the wheels of the Organization running smoothly. The theft, fraud, neglect of work, etc. are writ large in the 
conduct of the workman. For such an act of commission and omission, amount to wilful falsification of records, 
illegal gratifications, etc. the punishment of Dismissal from service cannot be said to be disproportionate; nor does the 
punishment call for interference by this Tribunal. One has to find out whether it is a case of no evidence or some 
evidence against. Secondly, one has to find out, whether material/ evidence available on record is sufficient for a 
prudent person to hold and arrive at a positive finding that the delinquent employee is guilty of the charges of 
misconducts levelled. In the instant case, on the one hand, management has led oral and documentary evidence before 
this Tribunal and on the other hand, the workman has completely failed to rebut the allegations levelled against him. 
Management has produced more than sufficient evidence from which it infers that the guilt of the delinquent 
employee is completely proved. That the alleged fraud has been committed by delinquent employee Vinod Parate. 


42. In the case of Mohammad Salim Siddique V/s State of UP and others 20] (2UPLBEC575) Allahabad 
High Court and Ajeet Kumar Nag V/s General Manager Indian Oil Corporation Ltd Haldia JT 2005 (8) SC 
425. The distinction between departmental enquiry and criminal proceedings has been drawn. The two proceedings 
i.e. criminal and departmental are indirect different. They operate in different fields and have different objectives. The 
object of criminal proceedings is to inflict appropriate punishment on offender as the purpose of enquiry proceedings 
is to deal with the delinquent departmentally and to impose penalty in accordance service rules. The rule relating the 
appreciation of evidence if the two proceedings if also not similar. In a criminal law burden of proof is on the 
persecution and unless the persecution is able to prove the guilt of accused beyond reasonable doubts, he cannot be 
convicted by the court of law. In departmental enquiry on the other hand penalty can be imposed on the delinquent 
officer on a finding recorded on the basis of preponderance of probability procedure with respect to standard to proof 
in criminal case and departmental enquiry are different. In the case of departmental enquiry, the technical rules of 
evidence have no application and the doctrine of proof of beyond doubt has also no application in the departmental 
enquiry. Criminal prosecution is launched for an offence for violation of a duty the offender owes to the society or for 
breach of which law has provided that the offender shall make satisfaction to the public. So, crime is an act of 
omission in violation of law or of omission of public duty. The departmental enquiry is to maintain discipline in the 
service and efficiency of public service. There would be no bar to proceed simultaneously with departmental enquiry 
and trial of criminal case. Looking to this law established by Hon’ble Supreme Court there is sufficient evidence on 
record to establish the guilt of the delinquent employee in this case the allegations alleged by the management. 
Therefore, in this matter penalty can be imposed on the delinquent officer on a finding recorded on the basis of 
preponderance of probability. 


43. The Hon’ble Supreme Court in B.C. Chaturvedi V/s Union of India (995) 6 SCC 749, has categorically 
held that judicial review is not an appeal on merits but is limited to scrutiny of the decision-making process; 
accordingly, Courts/ Tribunals cannot reappreciate evidence or substitute their own findings in place of those 
recorded by the competent authority. 


44, In the case of Om Kumar V/s Union of India, (200) 2 SCC 386, Hon’ble Supreme Court of India after 
considering the Wednesbury Principles and the Doctrine of Proportionality, has observed and held that the question of 
quantum of punishment in disciplinary matters is primarily for the disciplinary authority and the jurisdiction of the 
High Courts under Article 226 of the Constitution of India or of the Administrative Tribunals is limited and is 
confined to the applicability of one or other of the well-known principles known as Wednesbury Principles. 


45. In Depot Manager, A.P.S.R.T.C. V/s Raghuda Siva Sankar Prasad, Air 2007 Supreme Court 52, the 
Hon’ble Apex held as: 


It is also not open to the Tribunal and Courts to substitute their subjective opinion in place of the one arrived 
at the domestic Tribunal. In the instant case, the opinion arrived at by the Corporation was rightly accepted by 
the Tribunal but not by the Court. We, therefore, hold that the order of reinstatement passed by the Single 
Judge and the Division Bench of the High Court is contrary to the law on the basis of a catena of decisions of 
this Court. In such cases, there is no place for generosity or sympathy on the part of the judicial forums for 
interfering with the quantum of punishment of removal which cannot be justified. Similarly, the High Court 
can modify the punishment in exercise of its jurisdiction under Article 226 of Constitution of India only when it 
finds that the punishment imposed is shockingly disproportionate to the charges proved. 


46. In the case of Lucknow Kshetriya Gramin Bank (now Allahabad, Uttar Pradesh Gramin Bank) & Anr. 
V/s Rajendra Singh, (203) 2 SCC 372 in Paragraph |6 the Hon’ble Apex Court has observed and held as under: 


9530 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : AUGUST 22, 2026/ SHRAVAN 3], 948 [Part II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


“6. The principles discussed above can be summed up and summarised as follows: 


a. when charges(s) of misconduct are proved in an enquiry the quantum of punishment to be imposed in a 
particular case is essentially the domain of the departmental authorities. 


b. The courts cannot assume the function of disciplinary/ departmental authorities and to decide the quantum 
of punishment and nature of penalty to be awarded, as this function is exclusively within the jurisdiction of the 
competent authority. 


c. limited judicial review is available to interfere with the punishment imposed by the disciplinary authority, 
only in cases where such penalty is found to be shocking to the conscience of the court. 


d. Even in such a case when the punishment is set aside as shockingly disproportionate to the nature of charges 
framed against the delinquent employee, the appropriate course of action is to remit the matter back to the 
disciplinary authority or the appellate authority with direction to pass appropriate order of penalty. The court 
by itself cannot mandate as to what should be the penalty in such a case. 


e. the only exception to the principle stated in para 9.4 above, would be in those cases where the co-delinquent 
is awarded lesser punishment by the disciplinary authority even when the charges of misconduct were identical 
or the co-delinquent was foisted with more serious charges. This would be on the doctrine of equality when it is 
found that the employee concerned and the co-delinquent are equally placed. However, there has to be a 
complete parity between the two, not only in respect of nature of charge but subsequent conduct as well after 
the service of charge-sheet in the two cases. If the co-delinquent accepts the charges, indicating remorse with 
unqualified apology, lesser punishment to him would be justifiable.” 


47. The Hon’ble Supreme Court in Union of India & Others V/s. P. Gunasekaran, (205) 2 SCC 60,after 
relying upon its earlier judgements in the cases of B.C Caturvedi V/s Union of India & Other, (995) 6 SCC 
749; Union of India & Another V/s G. Ganayatham, (997) 7 SCC 463; Om Kumar & Other V/s Union of 
India, (200) 2 SCC 386; Coimbatore District Central Cooperative Bank Employees Assn. & Another, (2007) 4 
SCC 669; and chairman-cum- Managing Director, Coal India Limited & Another V/s Mukul Kumar 
Choudhuri & Other, (2009) 5 SCC 620; has held that authoritatively delineated the contours of judicial review in 
disciplinary matters. It has been categorically held that High Courts cannot re-appreciate evidence, cannot interfere 
with findings of fact if they are based on some evidence and cannot interfere with the quantum of punishment unless 
the same is shockingly disproportionate or vitiated by perversity, illegality, or procedural impropriety. The Hon’ble 
Apex Court emphasised that adequacy or sufficiency of evidence is beyond the scope of judicial review. 


48. Further the Hon’ble High Court of Gujarat at Ahmedabad in Rajendra G. Parikh V/s. Chief General 
Manager & Anr., C/SCA/2390/202 vide judgement dt. 2.07.2024, observed, as under: 


“The above-mentioned decisions of this Court give a clear idea of the limited scope of judicial review of the 
discretion exercised by the employer to impose the particular penalty on the delinquent employee. The 
Supreme Court has repeatedly emphasised that the High Courts cannot exercised appellate jurisdiction in such 
matters and substitute their opinion for the one formed by the disciplinary authority. It has been held that the 
punishment imposed by the competent authority cannot be modified. Substituted with a lesser penalty unless 
the court is satisfied that the same is grossly or shockingly disproportionate or is so unreasonable that no 
person of reasonable prudence would have imposed such punishment in the facts and circumstances of 
the case.” 


49. The act of putting Employee’s money to jeopardy speaks ill of the Honesty and integrity of the workman. 
The act of dishonesty or fraud or misappropriation lowers down the reputation of employer in public. The public 
loose their confidence in the company, which affects business and finally the national economy. That, the Hon’ble 
Supreme Court in Air-India Corporation, Bombay V/s. V.A. Rebellow and Ors., AIR 972 SC 343 observed 
that: 


“Once the employer has lost confidence in the employee and the Bonafide loss of confidence is affirmed, the 
order of punishment must be considered to be immune from challenge, for the reason that discharging the 
office of trust and confidence requires absolute integrity, and in a case of loss of confidence, reinstatement 
cannot be directed.” 


50. In Kanhaiyalal Agrawal and Ors. V/s. Factory Manager and Ors., AIR 200l SC 3645; the Hon’ble 
Apex Court laid down the test for loss of confidence to find out as to whether there was bona fide loss of confidence 
in the employee, observing that: 


“() the workman is holding the position of trust and confidence; 
(ii) by abusing such position, be commits act which results in forfeiting the same; and 


(iii) to continue him in service/ establishment would be embarrassing the inconvenient to the employee, or 
would be detrimental to the discipline or security or the establishment. Loss of confidence cannot be subjective, 


[भागवा--खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : अगस्त 22, 2026/ श्रावण 3, 948 953| 


based upon the mind of the management. Objective facts which would lead to a definite inference of 
apprehension in the mind of the management, regarding trust worthiness or reliability of the employee, must 
be alleged and proved.” 


Sl. The Hon’ble Supreme Court in Regional Manager, U.P.S.R.T.C, Etawah & Ors. V/s. Hoti Lal & Anr., 
(2003) 3 SCC 605, outlined the mode to be adopted for determining whether the punishment imposed by the 
disciplinary authority is shockingly disproportionate and observed as under: 


The court or tribunal while dealing with the quantum of punishment has to record as to why it is felt that the 
punishment was not commensurate with the proved charges. The scope for interference is very limited and restricted 
to exceptional cases. In the impugned order of the High Court no reasons whatsoever have been indicated as to why 
the punishment was considered disproportionate. Failure to give reasons amounts to denial of justice. A mere 
statement that it is disproportionate would not suffice. It is not only the amount involved but the mental set-up, the 
type of duty performed and similar relevant circumstances which go in to decision-making process while considering 
whether the punishment is proportionate or disproportionate. If the charged employee holds a position of trust where 
honesty and integrity are inbuilt requirements of functioning, it would not be proper to deal with the matter leniently. 
Misconduct in such cases has to be dealt with iron hands. Where the person deals with public money or is engaged in 
financial transactions or act in a fiduciary capacity, the highest degree of integrity and trustworthiness is a must and 
unexceptional.” 


52. That the Hon’ble Supreme Court in State Bank of India and Anr. V/s. Bela Bagchi & Ors., (2005) 7 SCC 
435, observed the following in Para 5: 


“5. A Bank officer is required to exercise higher standards of honesty and integrity. He deals with money of 
the depositors and the customers. Every officer/ employee of the Bank is required to take all possible steps to 
protect the interests of the Bank and to discharge his duties with utmost integrity, honesty, devotion and 
diligence and to do nothing which is unbecoming of a bank officer. Good conduct and discipline are 
inseparable from the functioning of every officer/ employee of the Bank. As was observed by this Court in 
Disciplinary Authority-cum-Regional Manager V/s Nikunja Bihari Patnaik (996) (9) SCC 69, it is no defence 
available to say that there was no loss or profit resulted in case, when the officer/employee acted without 
authority. The very discipline of an organisation more particularly a Bank is dependent upon each of its 
officers and officers acting and operating within their allotted sphere. Acting beyond one’s authority is by itself 
a breach of discipline and is a misconduct. The charges against the employee were not casual in nature and 
were serious. That being so, the plea about absence of loss is also sans substance.” 


53. Apex Court in Divisional Controller, N.E.K.R.T.C. V/s H. Amaresh reported in (2006) 6 SCC 87 
upheld the dismissal of the delinquent who had misappropriated Rs. 360.95/-. The court held that “the loss of 
confidence is the primary factor and not the amount of money misappropriated and that the sympathy or generosity 
cannot be a factor which is impermissible in law.” 


54. Recently the Hon’ble Supreme Court in the General Manager (P), Canara Bank V/s/ 
Ganganarasimhaiah, 2025 INSC 088 ruled as under: 


When a court is considering whether the punishment of “termination from service” imposed upon a bank employee 
is shockingly excessive or dis-proportionate to the gravity of the proved misconduct, the loss of confidence in the 
employee will be an important and relevant factor. When an unknown person comes to the bank and claims to be the 
account-holder of a long inoperative account, and a bank employee, who does not know such person, instructs his 
colleague to transfer the account from “dormant” to “operative” category (contrary to the instructions regulating 
dormant accounts) without any kind of verification, and accepts the money withdrawal from such person, gets a token 
and collects the amount on behalf of such person for the purpose of handing it over to such person, he in effect 
enables such unknown person to withdraw the amount contrary to the banking procedures; and ultimately, if it 
transpires that the person who claimed to be the account- holder was an impostor, the bank cannot be found fault with 
if it says that it has lost confidence in the employee concerned. A bank is justified in contending that not only the 
employees who are dishonest, but those who are guilty of gross negligence, are not fit to continue in its service. 


55. A disciplinary proceeding is not a criminal trial. The standard proof required is that of preponderance of 
probability and not proof beyond reasonable doubt. The principle of preponderance of probability in departmental 
enquiries has been succinctly summed-up by the Hon’ble Justice V.R. Krishna Iyer in State of Haryana and 
another V/s Rattan Singh (977) 2 SCC 49. To quote the unparallel and inimitable expressions: 


“In a domestic enquiry the strict and sophisticated rules of evidence under the Indian Evidence Act may not 
apply. All materials which are logically probative for a prudent mind are permissible. There is no allergy to 
hearsay evidence provided it has reasonable nexus and credibility. It is true that departmental authorities and 
Administrative Tribunals must be careful in evaluating such material and should not glibly swallow what is 
strictly speaking not relevant under the Indian Evidence Act. For this proposition it is not necessary to cite 
decisions nor text books, although we have been taken through case-law and other authorities by counsels on 


9532 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : AUGUST 22, 2026/ 8पसा२७0 ४७४ 3, 948 [Part II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


both sides. The essence of a judicial approach is objectivity, exclusion of extraneous materials or considerations 
and observance of rules of natural justice. Of course, fair play is the basis and if perversity or arbitrariness, 
bias or surrender of independence of judgement vitiate the conclusions reached, such finding, even though of a 
domestic tribunal, cannot be held good.” 


56. That in the departmental enquiry, the standard of proof required in a departmental order enquiry differs 
materially from the standard of proof required in a criminal trial. The Supreme Court in the cases Union of India V/s. 
Sardar Bahadur (972 SLR SC 355); State of Andhra Pradesh V/s. Shree Rama Rao (AIR 963 SC 723) and 
Nand Kishore Prasad V/s State of Bihar (978 (2) SLRSC 46), has held; “A disciplinarily proceeding is not a 
criminal trial and that standard of proof required in a disciplinarily enquiry is that of preponderance of 
probability and not proof beyond reasonable doubt, which is the proof required in a criminal trial.” 


57. It is worthwhile to refer to the decision of High Court of Gujrat at Ahmedabad in the case of C. S. Amin V/s. 
Assistant General Manager SBI, Region III & 2 reported in 206 Law Suit (Guj) 20I6 wherein the Hon’ble 
Court has observed as under: - 


“TI take notice of the fact that the inquiring authority has examined each and every charge levelled against the 
writ applicant including the documents produced by the Presenting Officer and came to the conclusion that all 
the charges were fully proved. In a departmental inquiry, the disciplinary authority is expected to prove the 
charges on preponderance of proportionality and not on proof beyond the reasonable doubt.” 


58. The Hon’ble Supreme Court of India in Orissa Mining Corporation Appellants and Another Vs. Ananda 
Chandra Prusty, (996) i SCC 600, (at Para 6), held as: 


“On a consideration of the totally of the facts and circumstances of the case including the nature of charges we 
are not inclined to interfere in the matter. The position with respect to burden of proof is as clarified by us 
herein above viz., that there is no such thing as on absolute burden of proof, always lying upon the department 
in a disciplinary inquiry. The burden of proof depends upon the nature of explanation and the nature of 
charges. In a given case the burden may be shifted to the delinquent officer, depending upon his explanation. 
For example, take the first charge in this case. The charge was that he made certain false nothing on account of 
which loans were disbursed to certain ineligible persons. The respondent’s case was that those nothing was 
based upon certain documents produced and certain records maintained by other employees in the office. In 
such a situation it is for the respondent to establish his case. The department is not expected to examine those 
other employees in the office to show that their acts or records could not have formed the basis of wrong 
noting’s made by the respondent.” 


59. It is also to be noted at this juncture that there is an essential distinction between burden of proof and onus of 
proof. Burden of proof lies upon a person who has to prove the fact and it never shifts, onus of proof on the other 
hand, shifts. Such a shifting of onus is a continuous process in the evaluation of evidence. For instance, in a suit for 
possession based on title, once the Plaintiff has been able to create a high degree of probability so as to shift the onus 
on the Defendant, it is for the Defendant to discharge his onus and in the absence thereof, the burden of proof lying on 
the Plaintiff shall be held to have been discharged so as to amount to proof of the Plaintiffs title, vide RVE 
VENKATACHALA Gounder V. Arulmigu Viswesaraswami and V.P. Temple, AIR 2003 SC 4548 (4558-59); 
(2003) 8 SCC 752. That once the misconduct is proved either in the enquiry conducted by an employer or by the 
evidence placed before a Tribunal for the first time, punishment imposed cannot be interfered with by the Tribunal 
except in cases where the punishment is so harsh as to suggest victimization. 


60. Learned counsel for the applicant has placed reliance on the following judgements: - 


i. (2009) 2 Supreme Court cases 570, Roop Singh Negi V/s Punjab National Bank and Others, Civil Appeal No. 
743 of 2008 


ii. (2022) 3 SCC , Abhay Jain V/s High Court of Judicature for Rajasthan and another, Civil Appeal No. 
2029 of 2022 


iii. 995 SCC Online Bom 434: (995) 2 LLN 720: 995 Lab IC 496: (997)  LLJ 206, Chandrakumar 
Madhukar Deshmukh and others V/s Board of Trustees of Port of Bombay and others. 


iv. AIR 200 SC (Supp) 753, Civil Appeal No. 893 of 2007, LIC of India and Anr. V/s Ram Pal Singh Bisen 
v. 969 AIR (SC) 983, Central Bank of India Ltd. V/s Prakash Chand Jain, Case No. 498 of 966 


vi. AIR 972 Supreme Court 330 Civil Appeal No. 254 of 966, M.s Bareilly Electricity Supply Corporation 
Ltd. V/s the workmen and others. 


vii. 2009 SCC Online P&H 6827, Diwan Chand V/s The Presiding Officer Labour Court, Amritsar and 
another, Civil W.P. No. 0899 of 988. 


Viii. AIR 2020 Supreme Court 4908, Arjun Punditrao Khotkar V/s Kailash Kushanrao Gorantyal and Others. 


[भागवा--खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : अगस्त 22, 2026/ श्रावण 3], 948 9533 


The above case laws are not applicable as the facts and circumstances are different with the present case in hand. 


6l. In view of the above discussions and deliberations, I am of the opinion that there are repeated transactions 
involving acts of moral turpitude committed by the workman which resulted into loss. So far, the proportionality of 
the punishment is concerned it is found that commission of grave misconduct of dishonesty, act highly prejudicial to 
the interest of the management and causing loss to the management have been proved against the workman. The 
misconduct reflects lack of integrity with the workman and lack of devotion towards duty. Keeping in view the 
seriousness of the charges proved a punishment cannot be said to be shockingly excessive or disproportionate. So, 
there is no scope to interfere with the punishment. Hence, the punishment of dismissal given with effect from 
08.08.20i2 to Shri. Vinod Anandrao Parate who was working as Chief Administrative Clerk at Hindustan Petroleum 
Corporation Limited (HPCL) Nagpur in imposing the penalty of dismissal from service is just fare and legal. On the 
basis of above findings and discussions reference is answered as follows. 


AWARD 


“The action of the disciplinary authority, Hindustan Petroleum Corporation, Nagpur, in imposing the 
penalty of Dismissal from service on Shri. Vinod Parate, Chief Administrative Clerk vide his order dt. 
08.08.202 is just, fair and legal. The applicant is not entitled to any relief.” 


A copy of this order be sent to both the parties as per the rules and file be consigned to the record room after 
due compliance. 


(Justice (Retd.) SHIV SHANKAR PRASAD, Presiding Officer

The Central Government, Ministry of Labour & Employment, New Delhi vide its order 
No.L-300/37/999-IR(M) dated 7.05.20l7 referred the following Industrial Dispute to this Tribunal 
for adjudication:- 


2. The schedule mentioned in that order is: 


“Whether the demand of ONGC Madras Port Contract Employees Union seeking regularization of 55 
contract workmen in the Principal Employer establishment of Oil and Natural Gas Corporation Ltd., 
Chennai listed in Annexure A as ONGC is Principal Employer is legal and justified? If not, to what 
relief the workmen are entitled to?” 


Facts of the case:- 


3. The Joint Secretary, Transport and Dock Workers Union and three others submitted a request before the 
Ministry of Labour and Employment, Government of India, New Delhi to refer the dispute before this Tribunal. The 
said representation was decided by the Government of India on the following terms:- 


The workmen were never employed by the ONGC, but were all employed by various contractors. No ID therefore, 
exists against M/s.ONGC Ltd. for their employment. Later on 07..!999 and Corrigendum dated 0/2000 was issued 
by the Central Government in the proceedings vide reference No. L-300]/37/l999-IR(M) dated 7.05.207. The 
ONGC filed a Writ Petition ४०.३|57 of 200l before the Hon'ble High Court of Madras to quash the order dated 
7..999 and corrigendum 00/2000 on the ground that the Petitioner had to transport materials and other supplies 
from on shore to off shore. The contract for stevedoring, shipping services, loading and unloading of materials and 
transport of materials by tempo, trucks, vans, trailers, etc. within a radius of 35 kms., from Madras Port and movement 
of materials by crane, fork lifts, etc. was awarded by calling for tenders from various entrepreneurs. The last of such 
contracts was entered into between the petitioner and M/s Pandian Transports for a period of one year commencing 
from |8.08.994 which was terminated with effect from 04.0!.!995. The union raised demands in respect of wages 
payable to them. The union and Transporter had entered into a settlement under Section !8 (l) of the Industrial 
Disputes Act on 29.08.994. On the termination of the contract between the petitioner and the contractor, the said 
Union made a demand for payment of earned wages for the month of December, 994 and 3 days in January, 995. 
The contractor had settled all the claims by 996. The Union raised an industrial dispute with respect to revision of 
wages of the workmen of the contractor. The establishment was also impleaded as a party. Conciliation proceedings 
took place. The ONGC took a stand that there was no privity of contract between the establishment and the members 
of the Union and it had no control over the said contract work. The Assistant Labour Commissioner (C) passed an 
order on 9.09.995 specifically stating that the dispute was not fit for adjudication as the workmen were never 
employed by ONGC, but were employed by various contractors and no industrial dispute exists against the ONGC. 
However, the Labour Ministry, Govt. of India passed an order dated 7..999 stating that industrial dispute exists 
between the ONGC management and their workmen. This order was subsequently revised by corrigendum dated 
3.0.2000. the ONGC establishment claimed that Labour Ministry’s order under reference for ID is invalid because 
there is no relationship of employer and employee between the ONGC and the workmen mentioned in the list 
Annexure-A — in the list of corrigendum. 


4. The Hon’ble High Court in Writ Petition No.2407 of 2000 has observed as under:- 


“It had been specifically stated that the petitioner was never the direct employer at any point of time which was 
accepted earlier on the same set of facts. It cannot be said that some new facts were brought to the notice of the 
Respondent (Labour Ministry Vs. Union of India) or that there was some other relevant consideration for referring the 
matter u/s.l0() of the Act. 


[भाग वा--खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : अगस्त 22, 2026/ श्रावण 3], 948 9535 


The power of the Respondent does not appear to have been exercised reasonably and in a rational mannet.... 
The order of reference shall stand set aside. The matter is remanded to the Respondent for consideration afresh.” 


3: This judgement was challenged by the ONGC Contract Employees Union in W.A. No.3622 of 2002 against 
the management of ONGC and 4 others. The Hon’ble Writ Appellate Court had observed that there is no force in the 
Writ Appeal and accordingly, the same was dismissed. Thereafter, the Labour Ministry, Govt. of India referred the 
present Industrial Dispute. 


6. As per the claim petition, the workmen are represented by the petitioner Union affiliated to Hind Mazdoor 
Sabha Sangh. The members of the petitioner union had been employed for more than 60 years doing handling job for 
the Respondent through various contractors. Even though the 2™ party had been changing contractors year after year, 
the members of the petitioner union continued in employment as such with the Respondent. The Respondent entered 
into a settlement with the contractors and names of the members of 7 Party are mentioned in the said settlement. 


7. It is further stated that the work in which Petitioner Union’s members were involved is perennial and 
permanent in nature and the Respondent instead of employing the workmen directly to do the said handling job 
engaged the members of the Petitioner through contractors in order to deny benefits of regularization of service and 
extension of various welfare status. When the members of the Petitioner union were employed through M/s.Pandian 
Transport for the Respondent, the erstwhile union raised industrial dispute regarding charter of demands and the said 
conciliation ended in failure on 3!.0.995. The members of the Petitioner gave notice of strike and struck work from 
04.0.995. the Respondent instead of negotiating and settling dispute took extreme step of terminating the contract 
itself in order to deny work to the members of the [7 party who lost even the status of contract workman. Thus, the 
Respondent revalidated the contract with M/s. Pandian Transport on 30.05.99 for a brief period of three months and 
the members of the Petitioner were again engaged for the said period of three months. The Madras Port Trust had also 
issued harbour entry permit passes to the members of the Petitioner union for carrying out contract work of the 
Respondent. 


8. It is also stated that in a similar situation, Hon'ble Supreme Court in the case of ONGC Vs Petroleum Coal 
Labour Union and others vide its order dated 7.04.205 in Civil Appeal No.3727 of 205 has directed the ONGC to 
regularize the service of the concerned workmen and to give back wages from the date of their entitlement. Therefore, 
the Petitioner prays to hold that demand of the Petitioner Union for regularization of 54 workmen covered by the 
order of the 20 Party is fully justified and direct the Respondent to reinstate the 54 workmen into service with full 
back wages, continuity of service with other attendant benefits. 


9. During the pendency of references, it is informed that !7 workmen died. It was requested by this Tribunal 
that legal heirs may be substituted on behalf of the deceased workmen . 


0. The learned counsel appearing on behalf of the workmen filed substitution petition only for l4 deceased 
workmen, but this petition is not supported by an affidavit and there was no request to set aside the abatement which 
had already took place on failure of the substitute of LRs within 90 days after the death of the concerned workmen. 
Therefore, this petition was dismissed. Therefore, the matter with reference to !7 workmen who died and their legal 
heirs are not substituted as abated. 


ll. The Respondent filed written submissions and stated that the workmen never employed by the respondent, 
but were employed by various contractors, no industrial dispute exists against the respondent for their employment. It 
was the contractors responsibility to handle the material with his own men and using his own supervisor to supervise 
the work. The Respondent did not have any control over such work. It was the total responsibility of the contractor to 
carry out the obligation as per the terms of contract with the respondent. There is no employer-employee relationship 
between the ONGC Ltd and members of the Petitioner union. 


2. It is further stated that members of the Petitioner union were not directly employed by the respondent and no 
identity card was ever issued for their employment. The claim made by the Petitioner union is not maintainable either 
in law or on facts and are not entitled to any relief whatsoever. It is also stated that the Petitioner union does not have 
any right or locus standi to represent the workmen of the contractors. The Petitioner union is not a recognized union 
by the respondent. The claim made by the petition union is barred by labour laws. The Transport & Dock Workers 
Union have arrived at a final settlkement with the contractors as evidenced by Memorandum of Settlement dated 
07.05.996 and the matter has become final. There has been no victimization or unfair labour practice on the part of 
the Respondent and no signature has been appended by the officials of the Respondent on the entry passes issued by 
Madras Port Trust and if any such signature, it would have been only the work carried out on a particular date on 
contract basis. 


3. In the case of ONGC Ltd. vs. Petroleum Coal Labour Union and Others , it relates to regularization of term 
based security personnel who were directly engaged by ONGC and not through contractors. Therefore, it is submitted 
that the facts involved in the present case on hand and the above cited case are not the same and thus, not applicable. 


9536

4. Heard the learned counsels appearing for either side and perused the records. 

495 The point that arises for determination is whether the members of the Petitioner union were workmen of the 


Respondent Corporation? 


6. This position can be ascertained after consideration of real nature of relationship. In the present case on hand, 
the following facts are admitted by the Petitioner Union in the claim statement as well as during the course of 
arguments that - 


(i) Contractor recruited the workmen; 
(ii) Salary and PF contribution was paid by the contractor; 
(iii) The contractor exercised disciplinary control and entered into agreement with the union. (This issue shall be 


discussed in later part of this Award). 


7. It is argued on behalf of the Petitioner that the contract was sham or camouflage to evade the labour laws. 
The Respondent ONGC had exercised direct supervision and effective control over the workmen, issued identity card 
and contractor was merely a name lender with no independent business. He relied on the document marked as Ex.W27 
by the Petitioner which is harbour entry pass issued by the Assistant Commandant of CISF. But, this document 
cannot be termed as identity card issued by M/s. ONGC Ltd. This document is mere an entry pass to enter in the 
secured area issued by security authority i.e, CISF. 


9538 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : AUGUST 22, 2026/ SHRAVAN 3], 948 [Part II—SEC. 3(ii)] 


8. Another document relied on by the Petitioner is Ex.W28, is an identity card issued by Secretary, Transport 
and Dock Workers Union and the same was affiliated to Hind Mazdoor Sabha. This document is also not a proof of 
document for employment of workmen in the office of the Respondent . It is argued on behalf of the Petitioner that 
Ex.W29 is attendance register. But this document is not issued by the Respondent ONGC Ltd and this register relates 
to Hind Mazdoor Sabha Sangh and not the attendance register of Respondent/Management. There is no other 
documentary evidence available on record which may suggest that the Respondent issued an identity card or provided 
any attendance register in favour of the workmen of the Union. 


9. Now, I have to consider Memorandum of Settlement Ex.W2. This document was carried out between the 
Transport & Dock Workers Union and M/s.Pandian Transport. Mr. Pandian, proprietor was the person who 
represented the management and General Secretary and Joint Secretary of the Union and 44 labourers were covered 
under this agreement. This agreement came into effect forthwith from 0.08.994 to 28.02.996. This agreement was 
between the contractor and workmen regarding salary, sick leave, casual leave, earned leave and allowances etc. is an 
important piece of evidence that workmen were employed by the contractor and not by the Respondent, because the 
workmen accepted the contractor as their employer, and it was the contractor who paid wages, EPF and other benefits 
and therefore, the contractor was their employer. 


20. Now, I have to consider the whether the contract arrangement is sham as argued by the learned counsel 
appearing on behalf of the Petitioner? 


2. There is no proof available on record which may suggest that M/s. ONGC Ltd. had selected or approved the 
workmen or the contractor had not recruited the workmen. There is also no evidence that any official of the ONGC 
directly supervised the day to work of the members of Petitioner Union or assigned duty or fixed working hours or 
grant leave or evaluated performance to the concerned workmen. There is no evidence on record that concerned 
workmen were under the disciplinary control of ONGC. The Petitioner union failed to submit any documents which 
may prove that ONGC has issued any warning to any of the employees of the Petitioner union or put them under 
suspension or passed any transfer order or termination order against them. There is no evidence that the contractor 
supplied labourers to the ONGC. There is clear evidence on record that contractor was provided with the contract and 
he performed the contract by engaging his own labourers. 


22. Even, during the course of oral evidence, the witness appeared on behalf of the Petitioner Union i.e., 
Secretary of the Union himself admitted in his cross examination that workmen were not appointed by the respondent 
directly. The relevant part of the evidence runs as under:- 


“It is true that our Union members were not directly employed by the Respondent at any point of time. It may 
be true that the contract between M/s. ONGC Ltd. and M/s.Pandian Transport were wholesome ...... 


It is true that our names may not appear on record of the ONGC. It is true that M/s.ONGC Ltd has no direct 
control over the workmen and they are under the control of M/s.Pandian Transport.” 


In view of the above discussion, the above point is decided against the Petitioner Union. 


23. Now, I have consider applicability of case law in M/s.ONGC Ltd. Vs. Petroleum Coal Labour Union & Ors 
(supra). On perusal of the facts of this case, it appears that corporation employed some workmen as security guards 
and security supervisors through contractors. However, vide Notification dated 08.2.976 issued by Government of 
India u/s.l0() of the Contract Labour (Regulation & Abolition) Act, 970 abolished the contract labour . The 
concerned workmen employed as per settlement arrived at between the trade union and the management of ONGC 
u/s.8() of the Act. On 20..982, the Respondent corporation passed a resolution by its policy decision to entrust 
the security work to the CSIF to protect their installations. The said resolution was sanctioned by the President of 
India on !6.2.985. Thereafter, the workmen concerned raised an industrial dispute claiming regularization of their 
services in the respondent corporation. The matter was referred to the Tribunal and taken as ID No.66/99! and 
passed Award dated 26.05.!999 directing the Respondent Corporation to regularize the services of concerned 
workmen. The said Award was challenged before the Hon'ble High Court of Madras in W.P No.l846 of 2000 and 
ultimately the Award was confirmed by the Hon'ble Supreme Court. 


[भागवा--खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : अगस्त 22, 2026/ श्रावण 3, 948 9539 


24. The facts of the present case on hand are totally different. In the case of M/s. ONGC Ltd Vs. Petroleum Coal 
Labour Union & Ors (supra), the Respondent engaged the workmen through contractors. But in the present case on 
hand, the workmen were engaged by the contractor himself for his own work and not for the Respondent 


establishment. 


29: The learned counsel appearing on behalf of the Appellant submitted a copy of certified standing order for 
contingent employees of the ONGC. But, this standing orders are applicable to the employees of ONGC. This issue is 
already decided that the workmen, members of the Petitioner Union were not the employees of the Respondent 
ONGC. 

26. The learned counsel appearing on behalf of the Appellant submitted a copy of Contract Labour (Regulation 
& Abolition) Act, 970 and stated that this Act abolished the supply of labour through contractor. But, in the present 


case on hand, the employees are engaged by the contractor himself for his own work. 

27. The Appellant filed the copy of following case laws :- 

(i) M/s.Steel Authority of India Vs. National Union Waterfront Workers & Ors (200l) 7 SCC [. 

(ii) Umesh T.N & Ors. Vs. State of Karnataka in W.P ]२०.।9588 of 2023; 

(iii) Mahanadi Coalfields Ltd Vs. Brajrajnagar Coal Mines Workers’ Union (2024) SCC online SC 270; 
(iv) Ranbir Singh Vs. S.K.Roy (2022) SCC online SC 52]; 

(५) Air India Statutory Corporation Vs. Union Labour India ((997) 9 SCC 377; 


(vi) Order dated 27.0!.2026 of Hon'ble Supreme Court in SLP (Civil) No.l292 of 2023 in M/s.Premium 
Transmission P. Ltd Vs. K.S.Rathod & Others 


28. I have perused all the case laws relied on and considered the applicability of these case laws to the facts of 
the present case on hand. In all these cases cited above employees were engaged by the management itself. But, in the 
present case on hand, workmen are engaged by the contractor for his own work which was awarded by ONGC to the 


contractor. Therefore, these case laws are not applicable to the facts of the present case. 
AWARD 


In view of the above discussion, the claim petition filed by the Petitioner Union is dismissed as devoid of any 


merit. Ordered accordingly. 
The Reference is answered accordingly. 
Record be consigned to the record room.

नई दिल्‍ली, 4 अगस्त, 2026 


का.आ. 4824.--औद्योगिक विवाद अधिनियम (947 का i4) की धारा ॥7 के अनुसरण में, केन्द्रीय सरकार 
मेसर्स आंध्र सीमेंट्स लिमिटेड, जेपी ग्रुप, विशाखा सीमेंट वर्क्स; मेसर्स एज़टेक सर्विसिज़ के प्रबंधतंत्र के संबद्ध नियोजकों और 
श्री रापर्थी सोमूनाइड के बीच अनुबंध में निर्दिष्ट केन्द्रीय सरकार औद्योगिक अधिकरण एवं श्रम न्यायालय, हैदराबाद, 
पंचाट (रिफरेन्स न.- 72/2048) को जैसा कि अनुलग्रक में दिखाया गया है, प्रकाशित करती है जो केन्द्रीय सरकार को 
सॉफ्ट कॉपी के साथ 4.08.2026 को प्राप्त हुआ था |

AWARD 


The Government of India, Ministry of Labour by its order No. L-2902/7/20i8-IR(M) dated 3.04.208 
referred the following dispute under section lJJO()(d) of the ID. Act, 947 for adjudication to this Tribunal between 
the management of M/s Andhra Cements Limited, and their workmen. The reference is, 


SCHEDULE 


“Whether there has been termination of the services of Sri Raparthi Somunaidu, S/o Chena Sanyasi, supplied by M/s 
Aztec services(contractor) by the management of Visakha Cement Works units of Andhra Cements Limited/Jaypee 
Group of company (principal employer) in October 20l0 as alleged by Sri Raparthi Somunaidu or Sri Raparthi 
Somunaidu, S/o Chena Sanyasi, by M/s Aztec Services (contractor) has on their own suddenly stopped working on 
0I.0.20i0 as alleged by the management of Visakha Cement Woks, Andhra Cements Limited/Jaypee Group of 
company (principal employer)? 


[भाग वा--खण्ड 3(ii)] भारत का राजपत्र : अगस्त 22, 2026/ श्रावण 3], 948 954] 


Whether the action of the management of Visakha Cement Works, Jaypee Group of company (present principal 
employer) in not considering Sri Raparthi Somunaidu, S/o Chena Sanyasi or services is fair, legal and justified? If 
not, to what relief the concerned workman is entitled and what directions (if any) are necessary in this regard? 


Whether the action of the present management of Visakha Cement Works in not paying benefits for the past services 
rendered to Visakha Cement Works by Sri Raparthi Somunaidu, S/o Chena Sanyasi is legal and justified? If not, to 
what relief the concerned workman is entitled and what directions are necessary in this regard?” 


The reference is numbered in this Tribunal as I.D. No 72/20i8 and notices were issued to the parties 
concerned. 


2. On the date fixed for appearance and filing of claim statement, Petitioner remained absent. Despite sufficient 
opportunity, Petitioner has failed to appear and did not file any claim statement. Hence, a No Claim Award is 
passed. The reference is answered accordingly. 


Award is passed accordingly. Transmit.
